ABSTRACT
Annular Core Research Reactor (ACRR) radiation outputs through vertical cavities have been
documented in two reports. External radiation exposure in the ACRR Facility (ACRRF) high
bay (HB) is evaluated using Monte Carlo N-Particle (MCNP). Part A presents the main results
including validation against measured doses for practical reactor configurations. In Part B, maximum
HB dose from the unshielded central cavity (CC), Fuel-Ringed External Cavity II (FREC-II), and
Neutron Radiography System (NRS) is calculated to inform the ACRRF safety basis. Maximum
 rem rem
dose is ≈1430 300  MJ or ≈40,930 hr for prolonged exposure to full-power, steady-state operation.
Expanded variance reduction (VR) techniques are employed to substantiate the analytical approach
utilized in Part A. Part B provides an analysis of—and remedy for—the minor statistical noise
observed in the beamline associated with the partially shielded (4” insert absent) CC. Part B also
presents supplementary results of beamline radiation features, neutron spectra, and leakage through
the NRS tube. MCNP calculation of aircraft dose over ACRRF—made practical by the expanded
 mrem
VR techniques—is minimal at ≈1–2 300 MJ . Finally, a verification study in Part B completes the
verification and validation (V&V) of the ACRR radiation output study.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Part B presents analyses that complement those in Part A [1]. Together, these two parts demonstrate
how MCNP can be used to forecast prompt HB dose through the ACRR’s vertical cavities, including
verification, validation, and sensitivity checks.
Part A provided overviews of the MCNP model and fundamental VR techniques (i.e., the surface
source write (SSW)-surface source read (SSR) approach). Part A also presented dose results for the
base case, along with validation comparisons to legacy and modern HB measurements. Part B
expands upon Part A, providing additional insights and completing the work’s overall V&V effort.
The purpose and goals of Part B are discussed in Section 1.
In Section 2, a verification study is presented using reactor shielding formulae from Rockwell’s
Reactor Shielding Design Manual [2]. The study demonstrates that simple hand calculations can
be used to understand and estimate prompt reactor dose emanating from the partially shielded
ACRR.1 In combination with published analytical tests on radiation shielding, the verification
study shows that MCNP, as implemented in the analyses, correctly models the fundamental physics
of particle transport through shielding ducts and penetrations.
Section 3 presents an analysis of maximum prompt HB dose to support updates of the ACRRF
safety basis. While unshielded CC dose results are in Part A, those values were derived from
rotational (ROT) fluence-to-dose coefficients, and the base-case model omitted radiation outputs
from the FREC-II and NRS.2 In contrast, the calculations in Section 3 employ antero posterior (AP)
fluence-to-dose coefficients and include contributions from the FREC-II and NRS; all three vertical
cavities are conservatively assumed to be unshielded. Simultaneous, unshielded outputs from the
three vertical cavities—combined with AP coefficients—yield a defensible, best-estimate calculation
of the maximum potential dose for the safety basis. The use of AP coefficients is defensible in the
beamlines because of the collimation of cavity radiation (see Section 5.3).
Table 1 summarizes the total effective dose (TED) results in Section 3.

 Table 1: Best-estimate maximum calculated HB dose for unshielded, FF cavities with coupled FREC-II

 † Calculated power levels are used rather than those indicated by reactor instrumentation. Full power is defined as 2.39 MW. One hour of
 full-power operation is equivalent to 8604 MJ (≈28.7× 300 MJ).

Despite the reduced core-level radiation environments of FREC-II, its larger cross-sectional area
increases radiation streaming to the HB. Overall, maximum doses in Table 1 exceed the unshielded
1
 In this work, “partially shielded” refers to a shield plug (SP) or other structure (e.g., NRS collimator) present in a
 cavity tube but with a significant streaming path (e.g., the 4” insert hole in the CC SP) that reduces shield efficacy.
 “Unshielded” denotes the lack of any shielding structure in the cavity tube. Nominal presence of reactor pool water
 remains.
2
 ROT coefficients were considered appropriate for the scope of Part A.

 11

doses in Part A by a factor of ≈2 (again, due to FREC-II and the use of AP vs. ROT coefficients).
Dose atop the unshielded NRS is minor relative to the unshielded CC and FREC-II.
Further MCNP analyses in Part B include the following.
  • Minor beamline statistical anomalies are present in SSW-SSR MCNP results. Increased statistical
 errors near regions of greatest dose are counterintuitive and warrant attention. In Section 4,
 additional VR techniques are investigated to address the statistical anomalies.
 – Key result: DXTRAN is effective in addressing the anomalies.
  • Additional features of beamline radiation are resolved using added VR techniques. Scattering
 of the CC beamline is assessed. These results are provided in Section 4.
 – Key result: Using DXTRAN, fine spatial (⪅1 cm grid) and spectral (up to 640 energy
 groups) resolutions (⪅5% bin error) of neutron fluence are attainable throughout the
 facility and on the roof. Scattering of the CC beamline (by the facility walls/roof
 and objects placed over the cavity) can significantly alter dose gradients. A 1” steel
 plate placed over the partially shielded CC increases dose around the reactor tank from
 mrem mrem
          ≈30 300   MJ to ⪆100 300 MJ .

  • In Section 5.1, partially shielded CC dose is juxtaposed with partially shielded (collimator
 in) NRS dose.
 – Key result: Dose directly above the NRS is ⪅1% of CC beamline dose.
  • In Section 5.2, aircraft dose calculations are provided using the added VR techniques.
 – Key result: Dose at 1000 ft directly overhead of ACRRF is ≈1–2 mrem for a 300 MJ
 mrem
 pulse. Dose at 2000 ft is ⪅0.1 300  MJ . Through the combined use of SSW, SSR, DXTRAN,
 weight windows (WWs), and high sampling (high NPS), TED statistical error is <1%
 even for small tally regions 2000 ft over the ACRRF.
Supporting information on reactor source modeling and dose conversion coefficients may be found
in the appendices.

 12

1 INTRODUCTION
The ACRR is principally a strategic radiation machine: it exists to generate strategic levels of
neutron and gamma radiation over large cavity volumes. Even at distances exceeding 20 ft, partially
shielded line of sight to the core’s high-flux regions produces a considerable radiological hazard that
reflects the machine’s radiation outputs. Parts A and B of this work document the application of
MCNP to directly evaluate the ACRRF dose environment from prompt reactor radiation streaming
up from vertical cavities.
Part B complements Part A, which provided validation of base case results. Part B begins with
a verification study demonstrating that the employed code and input models adequately resolve
the fundamental physics. Next, to inform the ACRRF safety basis, maximum unshielded dose
is assessed, which considers simultaneous radiation outputs from the ACRR CC, FREC-II, and
NRS. Part B also expands upon the MCNP methods from Part A, demonstrating that additional
VR techniques can yield improved statistics for finely resolved tallies. Finally, supplementary
results are provided. Supplementary results include partially shielded CC radiation dose concurrent
with leakage through the NRS tube, aircraft dose estimates, and other features of CC beamline
radiation.
Reviews of the MCNP model features and assumptions are provided in Section 1.1. Section 1.2
discusses radiation metrics.

1.1 Model Description & Methodology
Consistent with Part A, most of the MCNP calculations utilize version 6.2 [3]. During this work,
version 6.3.1 became available and was employed for supplementary calculations [4]. Evaluated
Nuclear Data File (ENDF) version 8.0 (ENDF/B-VIII.0) nuclear data is used in both parts of this
work. However, for materials lacking gamma-ray production cross sections (e.g., cadmium) in the
isotopic data, older elemental data is used instead.
Key assumptions and scope limits in the modeling are summarized here. Most of these items
reiterate material from Part A, which also has an expanded discussion on the MCNP model.
 • The MCNP model of the ACRR is sufficiently detailed to account for fundamental mode
 reactor neutronics and corresponding cavity radiation environment, consistent with characteriz-
      ation efforts [5, 6, 7, 8, 9].
 • A simplified representation of the ACRRF HB is used, which includes the shield plug (with
 removable 4” insert), reactor tank, pool, floor, walls, and roof.
 • A SSW-SSR approach is utilized on high performance computing (HPC) resources, which, when
 appropriately implemented, accounts for reactor output characteristics in the vertical cavities.
 Refer to Part A and developer publications for the essential impetus for its use [1, 3, 4, 10].
 Certain nuances of SSW-SSR techniques are discussed in Section 4.
 • Dose calculations are limited to prompt, reactor-driven, external neutron and gamma dose
 emanating from cavities through the reactor pool. Radionuclide contributions are neglected
 and outside the scope of this work. Other potential radiation sources in the ACRRF, such as
 sealed sources and radioactive experimental specimens, are neglected and outside the scope.
 A supplementary analysis considers leakage through the NRS tube.

 13

 • Radiation metrics in this report (see Section 1.2) reflect imposed reactor yields (e.g., per MJ
 and per 300 MJ), which are consistent with actual reactor powers and measured characterization
 data. Dosimetry scaled from or informed by reactor instrumentation likely entails discrepancies
 not reconciled in this work.

1.2 Radiation Metrics
TED in units of rem due to neutron and gamma radiation is the primary dose metric. Other metrics
such as neutron fluence, neutron spectrum, and tissue absorbed dose are examined for select cases.
Calculation of these metrics using MCNP is discussed below.
1.2.1 Reactor Yield Tally Multiplier
Radiation magnitudes are reported per MJ and for 300 MJ, which is a reasonably large yield for 1
hour of typical, anticipated operations. Strictly speaking, vis-à-vis KCODE-derived tallies, MCNP
calculations of prompt external dose make no distinction between pulse and steady operations.
Total dose for a large, 300 MJ pulse (or pulse-reduced-tail or transient rod withdrawal) is identical
to integral dose from a 300 MJ steady-state run performed at any power level.
To translate MCNP tallies (which are per source particle) to meaningful values, a main tally
multiplier (Cyield ) is applied.1.1 Prompt radiation outputs from operating reactors are driven by
neutrons, including gamma dose. Cyield is simply the total neutrons generated per MJ of reactor
yield and is evaluated via Eq. 1. ν̄ is the average number of neutrons per fission. Q̄ is the average
recoverable energy per fission.

 MJ

For 300 MJ of yield, the tally multiplier becomes ≈2.3749·1019 . This tally multiplier is implemented
on MCNP FM cards and may be combined with other constants. Cyield is consistent with previous
analyses of ACRR radiation outputs and published characterization work [5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 11, 12, 13,
14, 15].
1.2.2 Human Effective Dose
Fluence-to-dose conversion coefficients are utilized to calculate TED. The coefficients are particle-
and energy-dependent tables published by the International Commission on Radiological Protection
(ICRP) and the American Nuclear Society (ANS). Unique coefficient tables are available for
different phantom irradiation geometries—AP coefficients are generally the most conservative for
prompt reactor outputs and exceed ROT and isotropic (ISO) coefficients by a factor of ≈2.
Unless stated otherwise, results in Section 4 and Section 5 utilize ICRP-74 for neutron dose and
ANS-6.1.1-1991 for gamma dose [4, 16]. Maximum dose calculations in Section 3 utilize AP ICRP-
116 coefficients. [17]. Radiation streaming out of cavities is collimated: direct exposure to the

1.1
 MCNP tally results are per fission neutron when derived from KCODE calculations.

 14

beamline is analogous to AP irradiation geometry. However, positions outside the beamline might
be better suited by ROT or ISO tables.
The coefficients from ICRP and ANS publications are expressed in effective dose per fluence
 pSv · cm2 . To convert pSv to rem, a straightforward unit conversion is necessary (Eq. 2).

Note that “n” (for neutrons) in the units of Cf inal indicates that the reactor yield multiplier is

values. Separate neutron and gamma tallies in rem are summed to obtain TED.

Dose rate in MW·hr can be obtained by multiplying 300 MJ results by a factor of 12. 1 MW-hr is
equivalent to 3600 MJ (i.e., 12 large operations of 300 MJ each). For dose rate at full power (in
rem
 hr ), the factor is 28.7 (i.e., 2.39 MW × 12). Protracted exposure to high-power reactor operation
is a very conservative assumption—the associated dose rates may appear uncharacteristically high
in relation to ACRR operational experience.
1.2.3 Absorbed Dose
Absorbed dose in soft human tissue is calculated for select results. Tissue dose is evaluated using
a track-length tally with a simple tally multiplier and by integrating the product of fluence, total
cross section, and ENDF heating numbers. This approach permits estimating dose whether the
material is present or not in the model. The modeled tissue material is four-component soft tissue
defined by the International Commission on Radiation Units and Measurements (ICRU) [18].
Equation 4 provides the constant multiplier on the FM card for evaluating tissue dose. The tissue
 g
atom density (ρatom ) is 9.58·10−2 barn−1 cm−1 , and the tissue mass density (ρmass ) is 1 cm 3 [18].

1.3 Mesh Tally Figures
Mesh tally graphics are created using TA-V post-processing tools for MCNP [20]. A python script
extracts mesh tallies directly from the MCNP runtape (assuming it is HDF5-formatted), sums the
neutron and gamma doses while preserving total uncertainty, and generates vectorized graphic files.
More details may be found under the GitLab repository for MCNP Utilities.1.2

1.2
 https://cee-gitlab.sandia.gov/zgdodso/mcnp_utilities

 16

MCNP-generated figures are utilized to confirm the post-processed mesh tallies. MCNP-generated
figures have the benefit of superimposing the mesh tally results over geometry surfaces. These plots
are best obtained from NOTEK commands. For example:
mcnp6 z notek run=RUNTPE com=PLOTCOMMANDFILE plotm=OUTFILE
The resulting postscript files can be viewed or converted to PDF using various software.

1.4 Software Quality
MCNP is developed by Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL). It is government off-the-shelf
software and is listed on the Sandia safety software inventory database [21]. MCNP is developed
with modern version control (e.g., Git-based), V&V, and bug reporting1.3 and resolution, as
documented by the developers [4, 23]. TA-V at Sandia has a Safety Software Quality Assurance
Plan (SSQAP) for MCNP [24]. The SSQAP documents for MCNP provide a record of example
V&V results and document its acceptance for TA-V applications (including safety). Installations
of MCNP were built on Sandia HPCs and tested following developer guidance.
Parts A and B of this work satisfy the Sandia TA-V calculation procedure TAV–AP–025 [25].

1.3
 Via mcnp_help@lanl.gov or through the MCNP discourse forum [22].

2 VERIFICATION STUDY
A verification study demonstrates that the MCNP code is correctly modeling the basic physics of
potential HB exposures through vertical cavities. With validation provided in Part A, verification
completes the V&V of the ACRRF prompt radiation output study. First, simple reactor shielding
formulae from Rockwell’s Reactor Shielding Design Manual [2] are compared to MCNP predictions.
Next, verification tests for basic radiation transport and shielding are assessed using MCNP.
Published analytical answers to these tests are compared to MCNP predictions.
In Part A, it was determined that HB dose is dominated by neutron radiation. Therefore, the
verification study focuses on neutron radiation metrics.

2.1 Hand Calculation Analysis
The Rockwell shielding manual provides simple formulae to estimate neutron radiation exposures
for several reactor shielding configurations. For transmission through cylindrical ducts—which are
akin to unshielded or partially shielded ACRRF vertical cavities—the applicable formula is provided
by Equation 7.

 D2
 ϕoutlet = ϕinlet (7)
 8L2
Equation 7 is the Simon-Clifford equation. The derivation of the Simon-Clifford equation is based
on simple geometric considerations—no scaling or correction factors are present in Eq. 7 [26].2.1
ϕoutlet is the radiation metric (e.g., particle fluence or dose) at the exit of the duct. ϕinlet is the
corresponding inlet value. D and L are the duct diameter and length, respectively.
The following caveats apply to Eq. 7:
 • ϕoutlet represents uncollided neutron radiation only.
 • ϕinlet represents an isotropic source with uniform spatial distribution over a volume (or
 surface) of similar scale with the duct diameter.
 • Leakage through/around the shield is not considered.
 • Scattering and secondary radiation are not considered.
 • Given its simplicity, the formula only provides first-order estimates of uncollided radiation
 streaming through a long duct (L ≫ D).
Minor variations of Eq. 7 exist that employ terms and parameters to account for additional effects
(e.g., attenuation by internal duct material, scattering, bends, secondary radiation, anisotropic
sources, large source volumes, and added conservatism) [2, 26, 27]. The additional terms and
parameters must be gleaned from pre-tabulated values (with restrictive applicability), informed by
measurements, or calculated by radiation transport. A simple comparison between MCNP analyses
and the Simon-Clifford equation is presented instead.

2.1
 The key terms in this expression derive from the ratio of areas (or solid angles), obtained by dividing the
 characteristics of the circular-duct exit geometry by those of a hemisphere.

 19

2.1.1 Verified Scenarios
For the hand-calculation portion of the verification study, scenarios are selected spanning the system
configurations and cavities from Parts A and B of this work. These cases include:
  • CC output for standalone (no FREC-II or NRS output) and partially shielded ACRR.
  • CC output for unshielded coupled ACRR–FREC-II–NRS system (described further in Section 3).
  • FREC-II output for unshielded coupled ACRR–FREC-II–NRS.
  • NRS output for unshielded coupled ACRR–FREC-II–NRS.
Table 2.1 describes the four verified scenarios. Verification of CC radiation outputs is considered
for two distinct configurations: “configuration α” corresponds to the standalone ACRR case that
was the focus of Part A, and “configuration β” corresponds to a system configuration considered in
Part B (further described in Section 3). For configuration β, each of the three cavities’ respective
outputs are assessed.

measurements are available from published characterization efforts, these values generally reflect
core-center radiation rather than duct-inlet radiation of the cavity tubes. Hence, MCNP-calculated
values of the duct-inlet radiation are used. Parenthetical values are 1σ relative errors expressed as
percentages.

To obtain appropriate ϕinlet values for Eq. 7, tally locations were judiciously placed at the center

CC and FREC-II are set to 10 cm. The NRS tally-cylinder height is 2 cm, positioning it as high
as possible without overlapping any collimator cells, which may be filled with various materials.
However, as shown in Fig. 2.1c, the collimator is voided. Each tally cylinder has a 5 cm radius
(10 cm diameter), providing reasonable estimates of radiation in the central cavity and matching
the 4” (≈10 cm) diameter of the SP insert.

2.1.3          Outputs
Table 2.4 compares cavity-output values of neutron dose and fluence derived from Eq. 7 and system-
level MCNP modeling. For the α configuration, hand-calculated dose and fluence are within 10%
of MCNP predictions of partially shielded CC radiation. This agreement is exceptional considering
the simplicity of Eq. 7 and MCNP modeling uncertainty.
For the β configuration, hand-calculated doses for the unshielded CC, FREC-II, and NRS exceed
MCNP values by 20–40%. Equation 7 reliably captures trends. For example, both hand-calculated
and MCNP-calculated results indicate that the FREC-II dose is approximately 2.3× the CC dose.
This ratio is intuitive, given the cavities’ differing inlet radiation and cross-sectional areas.
Hand-calculated fluences for the β configuration exhibit greater discrepancy. Nevertheless, Eq. 7 is
capable of estimating fluence within a factor of 1.5–2× of MCNP predictions.

2.2 Kobayashi-Mura Benchmarks
MCNP is tested using three analytical benchmark problems created by Keisukue Kobayashi, Naoki
Sugimura, and Yasunobu Nagaya (“Kobayashi-Mura” for short) [28]. These problems were designed
to test three-dimensional (3D) transport of radiation through shielding and void regions. Exact
analytical benchmark solutions exist for each test, enabling direct comparison with neutron-transport
code predictions.
2.2.1 Kobayashi-Mura Test Problems
Test problem 1 (Figure 2.2) involves source and void regions completely surrounded by a shield.
Similar to the ACRRF radiation study at hand, test problem 2 entails radiation streaming through
a straight shielding duct, as shown in Figure 2.3. Test problem 3 provides a more-complicated
3D duct streaming configuration with bends, as shown in Figure 2.4. Each test has a uniform,
isotropic, volume source distributed over a small cube.

 22

 Figure 2.2: Kobayashi-Mura test problem 1

 Figure 2.3: Kobayashi-Mura test problem 2

 Figure 2.4: Kobayashi-Mura test problem 3

Each test positions the source region next to a void region. An arbitrary shield material surrounds

 23

most (or all) of the problem domain, which is the only material in each test. The source regions
are also composed of the shielding material, offering some self-attenuation. For the shield material
neutron cross sections, two treatments are defined (for six total Kobayashi-Mura tests to analyze):
 1. The shield is a pure absorber: 100% of collisions yield absorption.2.3
 2. The shield is a 50-50 absorber-scatterer: 50% of collisions yield absorption, 50% yield scattering.
2.2.2 Kobayashi-Mura Test Results
MCNP input decks for the Kobayashi-Mura benchmark tests are adopted from previous work [29].
The inputs utilize the default geometric modeling capabilities of MCNP (i.e., constructive solid
geometry) and prescribed cross sections based on test specifications [23, 28, 29]. Point estimates of
flux are provided by point detectors (F5 tallies), which are analogous to the partially deterministic
techniques discussed in Section 4 and Section 5. Alternative MCNP modeling approaches to the
Kobayashi-Mura tests are possible but not investigated in this verification study [30].
Both MCNP6.2 and MCNP6.3.1 are evaluated against the six Kobayashi-Mura tests. Because the
results are nearly identical, only MCNP6.3.1 figures are presented.
Ratios of calculated flux to benchmark flux (i.e., C
 B ratio) at various 3D coordinates are presented
in the following six figures:
 • Figure 2.5: problem 1 with pure-absorber shield
 • Figure 2.6: problem 1 with 50-50 absorber-scatterer shield
 • Figure 2.7: problem 2 with pure-absorber shield
 • Figure 2.8: problem 2 with 50-50 absorber-scatterer shield
 • Figure 2.9: problem 3 with pure-absorber shield
 • Figure 2.10: problem 3 with 50-50 absorber-scatterer shield
Each figure shows CB ratios from MCNP calculations performed by Sandia and LANL (from [23]).
Sandia-calculated fluxes for the tests are nearly identical to LANL-calculated fluxes, as shown by
the blue-colored C
 B ratios in the figures; the calculated values agree to the 5
 th digit.

In general, MCNP predictions are in excellent agreement with the benchmark solutions. In the
Kobayashi-Mura publications, deterministic transport predictions can disagree with the benchmarks
by factors of 1.2–2× [28]. MCNP predictions are generally within ≈1% (1.01×) of the benchmark
solutions, demonstrating the advantages of Monte Carlo (even when using point-detector tallies).
For difficult tally positions (e.g., deep in a shield region), the MCNP tallies exhibit greater stochastic
error and larger disagreement with the benchmarks (up to ≈10%). In the pure-absorber cases,
greater error is also present for the tally within the source region (i.e., at (x, y, z) = (5, 5, 5) cm)
due to minor statistical anomalies inherent to point-detector tallies.
In conjunction with the analyses in Section 2.1, the excellent agreement between calculated results
and benchmark solutions verifies that the MCNP code used in Parts A and B accurately represents
fundamental radiation-transport physics. Ipso facto, MCNP predictions in this work reflect the
direct application of human logic and our fundamental understanding of radiation transport physics.

2.3
 Not to be confused with a perfect-absorber shield, the pure-absorber shield permits leakage of uncollided radiation.

3 MAXIMUM PROMPT DOSE FOR SAFETY BASIS
A maximum prompt ACRRF reactor-driven dose calculation is presented to inform updates to the
ACRRF safety basis. For this purpose, the SPs are removed, and prompt radiation outputs from
the three major vertical cavities are evaluated simultaneously. Furthermore:
 • The ACRR CC is free field (FF) (other than the 32” pedestal) and unshielded.
 • FREC-II is coupled, FREC rods (FRs) raised, FF, and unshielded. A sensitivity case considers
 the FREC-II decoupled (tilted away) with its FRs lowered.
 • The NRS is coupled, collimator removed, and unshielded.3.1
 • AP ICRP-116 fluence-to-dose coefficients are utilized for both neutron and gamma radiation.
These calculations are still considered “best-estimate” rather than “bounding.” HB dose can be
further increased by additional assumptions that quickly become unrealistic (e.g., reactor operation
with a severely drained pool) in relation to this work’s scope. Consistent with other analyses in
Parts A and B, these calculations prescribe nominal geometric and material definitions. External
dose calculations are still limited to prompt neutron and gamma reactor outputs. The reactor pool
is modeled with a depth of 25 feet, providing effective shielding for radiation outside vertical cavity
beamlines.
The safety basis calculations employ the standard calculational approach—SSW-SSR with track
replication—used in Part A. No additional VR techniques (see Section 4 and Section 5) are applied;
future work could, however, investigate their use to improve statistical precision.

3.1 MCNP Model with FREC-II and NRS
The MCNP model of ACRRF is expanded to include major vertical cavities. FREC-II and a
detailed rendition of the NRS are incorporated into the MCNP model, with their vertical extents
taken from available drawings. MCNP input models of the NRS and FREC-II are from previous
Sandia modeling efforts [9, 12, 15, 31, 32, 33].
When coupled, FREC-II radiation magnitudes are 81th to 41th of those in the CC, depending on the
radiation metric [7, 9]. However, relative to the CC, the FREC-II tube is ≈4 feet shorter and has
4× greater area. Thus, despite reduced radiation at the core level, an unshielded FREC-II produces
greater prompt reactor-driven dose in the HB.
At the core level, NRS radiation magnitudes are lower than those of the coupled FREC-II and are
10–100× below those in the CC. Core-level NRS radiation is comparable to, but still lower than,
that in the decoupled FREC-II. Refer to Appendix A. The NRS tube’s square cross section has an
area ≈3× that of the CC. For completeness, unshielded radiation leakage through the NRS tube
is evaluated simultaneously with radiation outputs from the CC and FREC-II.
Because of its fuel allocation and high (yet still subcritical) standalone multiplication factor,
FREC-II has measurable reactivity worth when coupled to the ACRR, subtly perturbing the
coupled power distribution [12].3.2 When FREC-II is coupled and its FRs are raised, the power
distribution tilts toward the FREC-II, causing a slight decrease in CC radiation magnitudes—the

3.1
 Heavy water pillows are left in place under the assumption that they increase neutron fluence escaping the NRS
 tube.
3.2
 Including its fuel-followed FRs, FREC-II has 186 UZrH elements. It has a standalone keff ≈0.985 when its FRs
 are raised (poison out, fuel inserted). Its coupled worth is ≈50¢ [12].

 31

MCNP models and the SSW-SSR approach automatically consider these reactor neutronic effects.
Consequently, for HB-dose assessments, it is prudent to model the coupled FREC-II with raised
FRs to maximize radiation output through the external cavity. (Note: consistent with standalone
ACRR radiation outputs from Part A, the ACRR control rod (CR) bank is positioned near delayed
critical, and the safety rod (SR) and transient rod (TR) banks are raised.)

included. Figure 3.2 shows side views through the core midplane (approximately east-west through
the CC and FREC-II).3.3 FREC-II is shown in the coupled position in Fig. 3.1 and Fig. 3.2a, and
the east nickel plate is absent. Fig. 3.2b shows the side view again, but with FREC-II decoupled
and the east nickel plate present. Facility-level images through the FREC-II plane are shown in
Figure 3.3.

Mesh tallies over the ACRRF are provided in Figure 3.6 for the unshielded ACRR–FREC-II–NRS
 mrem

 rem

Figure 3.7 shows relative errors over the mesh tallies. Mesh tally errors are less than 10% over
the HB floor space, including directly over the cavities. On the mesh spatial resolution (2.5 cm),
statistical anomalies are apparent in each of the cavity beamlines. Similar—but less drastic—
anomalies were identified in the base case results in Part A, which are further investigated in
Section 4 of this report.
Tally sampling over larger cells (i.e., those used in Table 3.1) mitigates the beamline anomalies.
Even over the fine mesh resolution of Fig. 3.6, the scattered radiation field is well-resolved by
the SSR track-replication process. Refer to Part A (Section 1.1) [1] and Section 4.1 in this report
for explanations of SSR track replication and beamline statistical anomalies. The safety basis
study is focused on resolving maximum dose atop each cavity over 20-cm diameter tally spheres.3.5
Statistical metrics are acceptable over these tally spheres, including the code-reported errors listed

4 ADDITIONAL VR TECHNIQUES
MCNP results utilizing additional VR techniques are provided for the partially shielded configuration
with the 4” insert removed and the 44” lead-boron bucket (LB-44) bucket in the reactor cavity.
These analyses corroborate the base case results (from Part A), demonstrate that the SSR-SSW
technique is the appropriate approach to the HB dose problem, and show that additional VR
techniques offer focused improvements. Since SSW-SSR is itself a VR technique, “additional VR”
refers to partially deterministic methods (DXTRAN) and WWs.4.1 These schemes have advantages
and disadvantages compared to the base approach, and some aspects of the VR results must
be scrutinized. Observations and deductions in this section are limited to the partially shielded
configuration where the SP is present without the 4” insert, and the LB-44 bucket is in the CC.

4.1 VR Scheme Inspiration and Overview
In the SSW-SSR results in Part A and Section 3, statistical anomalies exist in the HB beamlines.
In fact, statistical error can be greater in the beamline than the nearby HB airspace. Increased
statistical errors in regions of greater dose are counterintuitive and warrant attention.
This section offers an explanation of and a solution to the beamline statistical anomalies. As further
explained below, the anomalies are due to weight fluctuations integral to the SSW information from
the KCODE calculation. SSR track replication (through high NPS) alone cannot alleviate the weight
fluctuations. However, track replication does improve statistics away from the beamline—tally
errors can be reduced until the weight variance in the SSW information dominates [4]. Additional
VR techniques, including DXTRAN and WWs, can resolve the beamline anomalies and dramatically
improve calculational efficiency (i.e., reduced code-reported error for a given computational expense).
4.1.1 Weight Fluctuations
Particle weight is a tally multiplier in MCNP. For a given phase-space of a problem, particle
weights should ideally be similar [35]. Weight fluctuations arise from rare particles that have (i)
survived many collisions, (ii) streamed uncollided through a small opening (while most others have
undergone several interactions), or (iii) the consequence of a VR scheme.
SSW tracks can be replicated in the SSR calculation with different random number seeds. Afterward,
the particles have the opportunity to undergo unique collisions and transport. In this way, statistical
errors away from the beamline are significantly improved by SSR track replication (e.g., after
scattering with the roof). However, the initial streaming of the high-weight track remains. On
mesh tally plots, these tracks appear as ray-like lines of anomalously high fluence, dose, and relative
error.4.2
Potential workarounds to the SSW weight fluctuations were considered:
 • Employ DXTRAN and WWs to suppress the weight fluctuations (presented in this section).
 • Reduce the weight fluctuations written to the SSW through brute force (i.e., larger KCODE SSW
 calculations) or multiple SSW-SSR steps.

4.1
 Most MCNP calculations (even KCODE) use some form of VR such as survival biasing.
4.2
 The inability of SSR track replication to alleviate the problem is analogous to splitting particles across an expanse
 of air or void: statistically, each particle is destined to stream across the void, and splitting increases computational
 expense without improved sampling over the expanse.

 45

  • Do not use SSW-SSR and ignore source uncertainty. Tally the cavity leakage and manually
 construct an SDEF source.
The brute-force approach of running very long KCODE calculations is ineffective when relatively few
histories produce tracks crossing a distant and partially shielded surface. For instance, typical
KCODE SSW calculations for this work utilized 10 billion active histories, of which only ≈5 million
histories (0.05%) produce tracks crossing the specified surface. Thus, ≈99.95% of KCODE effort is
spent tracking particles that will never contribute directly to prompt HB dose through the CC.
The SSW-SSR approach is used primarily because it focuses on—and replicates with distinct random
number seeds—only the particles that have a statistically meaningful probability of escaping upward
through the CC.
Results using manually constructed sources are presented in Section 5.3. The manual source
approach has limitations that offer credence to the (mostly automated) SSW-SSR approach. For
example, gross assumptions in source angular dependence can change HB dose predictions by an
order of magnitude. Nonetheless, the manual approach substantiates key trends in the SSW-SSR
results and could be refined in future work.
Figure 4.1 illustrates the SSW weight fluctuations emanating from the partially shielded CC. This
figure demonstrates that track SSR replication cannot mitigate the problem, whereas DXTRAN-WWs
provide significant relief. The histograms represent unweighted neutron current (particle weights
via F1:N tally) exiting upward out the CC. A series of SSR calculations are depicted with different
levels of surface-source replication. The blue histogram replicates the surface-source information
≈100× for an effective SSR NPS of 4.4x108 histories. 1000× replication is executed by the green
histogram (NPS=4.4x109 ), followed by 10,000× in the orange histogram (NPS=4.4x1010 ). Finally,
the red histogram has 40,000× replication of the surface source for 1.8x1011 histories.

 Tally score (weight)
 Figure 4.1: Tally score weights

Although SSR replication increases the absolute number of scores and correspondingly decreases
their median weight, the bimodal score distribution, including the anomalous high-weight particles,

 46

remains. Despite the increased sampling, the number of high-weight particles contributing to the
score remains constant. The rarity, weight, and energy of these particles, which are important for
dose, cause the beamline statistical anomalies.
The black histogram in Fig. 4.1 utilizes SSW-SSR-DXTRAN-WWs, as elaborated in Section 4.1.2 and
Section 4.2. Except for the DXTRAN case, a bimodal distribution of tally scores exists: regardless
of the degree of surface-source replication, a constant number of anomalous high-weight particles
score, while the remainder have particle weights that are 2–5 orders of magnitude lower. Application
of DXTRAN disrupts this paradigm by suppressing high-weight scores and eliminating the bimodal
tally-score distribution. The PDF slope metric for tally scores using DXTRAN is generally “perfect”
at ≈10 (see the discussion in Section 3.2.1). The DXTRAN scores in Fig. 4.1 exhibit a relatively long
tail of low-weight scores, which is partially suppressed by application of the DD card; still, DXTRAN
performance might be improved by refining DD and DXC inputs.
A portion of the rare, high-weight particles also have high neutron energies, exacerbating the
beamline anomalies in dose mesh tallies. Figure 4.2 shows the energy dependence of effective
neutron dose, expressed as a cumulative distribution function (CDF) histogram. The blue CDF
in Fig. 4.2 is similar to the F1 tally used for the blue distribution in Fig. 4.1, except that it is
weighted by ICRP-74 ROT coefficients. The black CDF is the same tally using DXTRAN-WWs. The
energy bins are taken from ICRP-74. Effective neutron dose is dominated by neutrons with energies
≥0.1 MeV (100 keV), which is a characteristic trend in radiation environments with fast-neutron
fields. Due to weight fluctuations in the surface source, the statistical uncertainty in fast-neutron
dose contributions is notable, especially when tallied over fine meshes. With DXTRAN-WWs, the
uncertainty in the fast-neutron contributions has been markedly reduced. The following sections
extend this result to facility-level mesh tallies.

Table 4.1 lists the total scores of the F1:N tally in each case. This table demonstrates that the
histograms from Fig. 4.1 all yield the same result despite the different score distributions. These

 47

values can be physically translated to the number of neutrons exiting the CC using Eq. 1. Without
DXTRAN, the weight variance inherent in the surface source prevents SSR track replication from
reducing the error in the F1 tally below 1.85%. The use of DXTRAN and WWs significantly reduces
the tally error to 0.07%. The total tally scores are statistically identical at 2σ.

4.1.2 DXTRAN-WW Approach
DXTRAN and WWs are applied to the partially shielded CC HB dose problem, which is essentially a
shielding duct-streaming problem.4.3 SSW-SSR and track replication are still used to characterize the
reactor output source. In addition to corroborating the base-case results obtained using SSW-SSR
alone, the added VR techniques improve the statistical resolution of finely resolved tallies while
alleviating beamline anomalies. Even thousands of feet from the facility, fine spatial and spectral
tallies are feasible using DXTRAN and WWs.
While an in-depth review of VR techniques is beyond the scope of this report, developer guidance
suggests SSW, SSR, and DXTRAN can be used to address the beamline anomalies [4, 10, 36, 37]. WWs
are used in concert with these techniques to temper weight fluctuations in the DXTRAN contributions.
Other VR techniques are selectively used to improve calculations.4.4
The basic DXTRAN-WW approach is as follows:
 1. Execute KCODE SSW calculation.
 a. ≈10 billion active KCODE histories are executed to resolve fundamental-mode neutronics
 and the surface source.
 b. SSW locations below the SP generally allow for more efficient SSW and SSR calculations.
 2. Execute initial SSR calculation with DXTRAN and mesh-based statistical WW generator.
 a. Regions behind the SSW location are specified as IMP:N,P=0 or removed entirely from
 the model. Transport effects of these regions are integral to the SSW.
 b. Define mesh-based WWs with ≈1000 total zones.

4.3
 Analogous classic MCNP VR example problems include the oil well-logging problem, the fusion shielding problem,
 and Thomas Booth’s “class problem” [36, 37]. The criticality accident alarm primer from LA–UR–12–25545 [10]
 and the shield penetration problem from LA–UR–17–22351 [38] are also good examples.
4.4
 The DD and DXC cards can improve DXTRAN performance, suppressing extremely low-weight (and unimportant)
 contributions. ESPLT and a higher photon cutoff are also used. The rouletting of low-energy neutrons deep in the
 reactor pool can speed up calculations without impacting dose, which is dominated by fast neutrons. A higher
 photon cutoff (e.g., 5–10 keV) prevents the tracking of low-energy x-rays that are unimportant for dose.

 48

 c. For subsequent calculations, WW values are automatically estimated by the code using
 the statistical generator. For this work, the WW scheme optimizes weights for a neutron
 tally in the HB. Ideally, the optimization of the neutron tally is correlated with improved
 overall HB dose statistics, including gamma dose.4.5
 d. Define a neutron DXTRAN sphere. A photon DXTRAN sphere can also be defined to improve
 metrics that are more sensitive to gamma dose, such as TLD-400 or silicon dose.
 3. Execute additional SSR calculations using better-resolved WWs as necessary.
 a. 2–5 WW iterations can be beneficial.
 b. As necessary, adjust DXTRAN, ESPLT, DD, and DXC inputs to improve efficiency.
 c. Increase sampling for final results. Final MCNP calculations run ∼1012 histories.
A small DXTRAN (13.34 cm radius) is defined directly inside the cavity at the pool surface. Figure 4.3
depicts the MCNP model geometry used in the VR calculations, which is mostly identical to the
base case model (from Part A) except for some additional tally spheres and a tissue phantom placed
7.2 ft west of the CC. The phantom is 223 lbs of four-component ICRU soft tissue [18]. The tissue
phantom helps ascertain local radiation field and shadowing effects of a person standing in the
HB.

 Neutrons escaping the CC undergo local collisions that create gamma rays, contributing to HB gamma dose. WW
 optimization for both neutrons and photons (e.g., using a F6:N,P tally) was not as effective.

4.2 DXTRAN-WW Results
Figure 4.4 shows TED for partially shielded operation, calculated using the SSW-SSR-DXTRAN-WW
method. This mesh tally has a reasonably fine grid, with 2.5 cm zones in the east-west and
elevation directions. Dose magnitudes are averaged over a 10.16 cm (4”) segment in the model’s
south-north direction (into and out of the page in this figure) to improve statistics and aid in
visualizing gradients. The abscissa is the east-west position relative to the cavity center. Values
less than zero are east of the cavity, toward the shielded cells. Physically, this view corresponds to
that of an observer north of the facility looking south toward ACRRF. The ordinate in Fig. 4.4 is
the elevation above the HB floor.
The dose magnitudes in Fig. 4.4 are contingent on a PSC value of 15.9. As explained in Appendix A.3,
when combined with partially deterministic methods, SSR calculations entail an approximate probab-
ility that surface-source particles are emitted into a specified angle, which the user must provide
(i.e., PSC on the SSR card).

Fig. 4.5b highlights dose gradients around the reactor tank, which is denoted by the dashed white
circle. TED is on a linear scale with units of mrem in Fig. 4.5b. Given the offset placement of
 mrem
the ACRR in the reactor tank, nearby dose is around 30 300 MJ and is slightly greater to the west
side of the tank. Note that these figures ignore potential outputs from FREC-II and the NRS
tube—refer to Section 3 and Section 5.1 for studies that include those cavities. Due to radiation
scattering throughout the (as-modeled) facility, Fig. 4.4 and Fig. 4.5 show that dose is relatively
uniform at lateral positions >5 ft from the CC. Dose directly behind the phantom is about half the
dose facing the cavity. Standing behind a person in the HB would reduce one’s dose, but scattering
around the facility, which is simplified and largely empty in the model, may reduce the efficacy of
such local “shielding.”
The chief physical effects of the facility—the locations and dose characteristics of the cavity, floor,
walls, shielded cells, roof, and the tissue phantom—are visually distinguishable in Fig. 4.4 and
Fig. 4.5. These features will be discussed in subsequent sections. Efficient sampling using the
SSW-SSR-DXTRAN-WW method yields excellent spatial resolution in the cavity beamline. Statistics
≥10 ft laterally from the cavity are also acceptable.

 51

There is added value in extracting the mesh tallies directly from the MCNP runtape. MCNP-
generated figures of mesh tallies have the benefit of superimposing the mesh tally results over
geometry surfaces. Figure 4.6 depicts MCNP-generated mesh plots of neutron (Fig. 4.6a) and
gamma (Fig. 4.6b) effective doses for the same view plane as Fig. 4.4. Model surfaces including
tally spheres are displayed. These figures suggest the mesh tallies are being correctly processed.

 (a) Neutron effective dose (b) Gamma effective dose

 Figure 4.6: MCNP-generated mesh tallies of neutron and gamma dose using DXTRAN and WW

In comparing Fig. 4.6a and Fig. 4.6b, neutron effective dose is 50-100× greater than gamma dose
in the beamline and throughout the facility. The photon mesh tally exhibits greater statistical
noise despite the use of a photon DXTRAN sphere—this noise is exacerbated if the photon DXTRAN is
removed. The mesh-based WWs in this calculation were optimized for a neutron tally. For TED,
the greater gamma dose error is inconsequential since neutron dose dominates. Tally metrics more
sensitive to gamma dose (e.g., in silicon and TLD-400 material) might benefit from WWs optimized
for a photon tally.
The importance of using WWs in combination with DXTRAN is demonstrated in Figure 4.7, which
shows TED relative error with and without WWs. Both cases have very low (<1%) relative error
in the beamline. When using WWs, error remains ⪅5% within 5–10 ft of the beam. Without
WWs, error increases to 5–10% at greater lateral distances (>5 ft) from the CC. Vertically, error
near the beamline remains below 10% well above the HB floor. Comparing Fig. 4.7a and Fig. 4.7b,
the use of WWs significantly improves statistics outside the beamline. In both cases, non-DXTRAN
particles, which may have undesirable weight fluctuations, that reach the sphere are terminated
at the sphere surface; their dose impact occurs through DXTRAN contributions at source emission
and/or collisions. This abrupt termination of these particles is also apparent upon close inspection
of the dose contour plots.

4.2.1 Comparison to Base Case
Overall, the dose magnitudes and gradients calculated using DXTRAN-WWs agree well with results
from Part A that use only SSW-SSR. Figure 4.8 and Figure 4.9 compare TED and relative error,
respectively, between the base case and DXTRAN-WW results. The effectiveness of the DXTRAN-WWs
approach in suppressing weight fluctuations is apparent in these figures, and the beamline anomalies
have been extinguished. More importantly, the magnitudes and trends in the HB dose field are in
good agreement, demonstrating that the beamline statistical anomaly does not detract from the
underlying SSW-SSR results.

 Figure 4.9: Suppression of weight fluctuations by DXTRAN and WW VR techniques

As established in Part A and Figure 4.8, the HB radiation field is anisotropic. TED in the beamline,
 rem
when averaged over a 20 cm (7.9”) diameter tally sphere, is ≈140 300 MJ . Peak dose within a 4”
 rem
region over the CC, corresponding to the 4” insert hole, is ≈200 300 MJ . Just 1 ft away from the CC
centerline (i.e., in the east, west, south, or north directions), dose is 3 orders of magnitude lower
than the peak. Gradients in the vertical direction are more gradual. Even after scattering with the
roof, the radiation field is biased in the vertical direction. In fact, the collimated radiation beam
persists 1-2 thousand feet above the HB roof (see Section 5.2).
Table 4.2 compares dose from KCODE results, results from Part A that used SSR-SSW, and DXTRAN-
WW results. Neutron, gamma, and TED values are provided atop the cavity and 2.5 ft above the
roof. The KCODE and base case results are in good agreement for all three metrics, demonstrating
the SSW-SSR approach is capturing the true (model) reactor source. Statistically, neutron doses
from the DXTRAN-WWs results are in good agreement with those from the other techniques while
yielding much lower errors. However, gamma dose is overpredicted in the DXTRAN results, which is
an artifact of the PSC parameter when using DXTRAN in SSR calculations. See Appendix A.3 for more
information on this matter. The DXTRAN-WW method produces much lower statistical errors.

4.2.2 Facility-Level Dose Observations
Excellent spatial resolution of TED throughout the as-modeled facility is achieved through the
combined SSW-SSR-DXTRAN-WW approach. This section analyzes facility dose fields on the scale of
feet, while Section 4.3.1 examines beamline features within inches of the CC centerline.
Figure 4.10 shows a one-dimensional slice from Fig. 4.5, which has 1 cm lateral resolution, with
TED plotted as a function of east-west position relative to the CC. This figure examines the beam
gradient within ±5 ft of the CC. The south-north profile is very similar to Fig. 4.10 in the facility
model used here, which neglects the NRS and FREC-II. Statistical errors are quite small due to
the VR techniques, but are included as ±1σ error bars.
Immediately above the cavity, the highest dose is localized to a four-inch-diameter beam correspond-
 rem
ing to the 4” insert hole in the shield plug. Dose within this apogee is ≈200 300 MJ and is relatively
flat, suggesting that the 1 cm mesh sufficiently resolves peak dose. Just beyond 4”, dose sharply
decreases by a factor of 15× over a distance of 1 cm (0.39”). Rem-level dose persists out to a
diameter of 10”, corresponding to the outer diameter of the upper CC.
The partially shielded beamline slowly diverges with a conical half-angle of ≈1 degree. Neutron
collisions in the HB air, upper shield plug, cavity structures, and walls disperse neutron and gamma
radiation throughout the facility. The scattered radiation field produces much lower dose (<1 rem
at a foot from the CC) compared to the beamline. The horizontal line in Fig. 4.10 indicates the
5 rem annual DOE occupational dose limit. Outside the beamline, reduced reactor yield or limited
exposure time would further decrease exposure, providing comfortable margins below 5 rem.

dose depression in Fig. 4.11b: dose behind (west of) the phantom is reduced by a factor of two.

4.3 Partially Shielded Beamline Features and Sensitivities
A close examination of beamline radiation is provided in this section using the SSW-SSR-DXTRAN-
WW method. Model sensitivities are also evaluated to frame comparisons to measurements.
Hypothetical “one-off” cases are considered involving hypothetical objects placed over the CC that
increase beamline scattering throughout the facility. Variations in facility wall and roof modeling
treatments are also considered that influence the backscattered radiation field in the ACRRF HB.
These analyses are limited to the partially shielded and standalone ACRR configuration, in which
LB-44 is located in the CC, the SP is present, and the 4” insert is absent.
4.3.1 CC Beamline Radiation Gradient
A focused analysis of radiation features near the CC beamline is provided to aid comparison to
measurements. Improved statistics in the VR calculations allow beamline radiation gradients to be
resolved on tally meshes with lateral spacing of 1 cm (0.4”).
Figure 4.13 and Figure 4.14 summarize the key features of the calculated beamline dose per MJ of
partially shielded reactor operation. Positions less than zero are east of the CC towards the shielded
cells. Positive positions are west of the CC. Fig. 4.13 shows TED values using various tables of
fluence-to-dose conversion coefficients. Note that TED values derived from ICRP-74 neutron tables
make use of ANS–6.1.1–1991 gamma tables for the gamma component of TED (generally ≈1% of
TED). Fig. 4.14 has total neutron fluence on a separate y-axis. Doses in rad[material] are presented
for soft tissue and TLD-400 (CaF2 :Mn).
The following deductions are drawn from Fig. 4.13 and Fig. 4.14.
  • The beamline dose apogee ranges from 0.4 rem rem
 MJ to 1 MJ .

 – ROT dose tables (the default) yield TED values between those of AP and ISO tables.
 AP doses are double the isotropic doses.
 – Corresponding to the 4” insert hole, the dose profile is flat within 2” of the CC centerline.
 – Corresponding to the 10” cavity diameter, dose decreases gradually to 0.1–0.3 rem  MJ at
 distances 4–5” from the centerline. Beyond this extent, there is a rapid decrease in dose
 below 0.01 rem
 MJ .

 – ICRP-74 and ICRP-116 tables yield very similar dose for a given irradiation geometry
          (e.g., AP).
  • Tissue absorbed dose is ≈0.1 rad[tissue]
 MJ .
  • Peak total neutron fluence is ≈7x107 cm2n MJ . Averaged over a 20-cm diameter sphere, neutron
 fluence exiting the cavity is roughly 5x107 to 6x107 cm2n MJ . The neutron spectrum exiting
 the cavity is similar to the characterized LB-44 spectrum, as discussed in Section 4.3.2.
  • TED, absorbed dose, and neutron fluence all decrease in magnitude by 2–3 decades outside
 the 10” envelope of the upper cavity.

4.3.2 Neutron Spectra
DXTRAN-WW results for statistically resolved neutron spectra are presented using the NuGET [39]
89- and 640-group energy structures. Neutron spectra with 89 and 640 group structures are
depicted in Figure 4.15. The additional variance reduction has produced excellent resolution over
the meaningful parts of these spectra. Appendix C provides tabular output of the 640 group
spectrum.

 Energy [MeV]
 Figure 4.15: Partially shielded CC output neutron spectra with 89 and 640 energy groups

Figure 4.16 shows calculated neutron spectra at different lateral positions in the HB—except for
one location on the floor west of the reactor tank, these positions are at the same elevation as the
CC top (about 4 ft above the floor). Most bins have relative errors less than 5%, and thus the ±1σ
error bars are nearly imperceptible in Fig. 4.16. The neutron spectra are presented as normalized
fluence per unit lethargy, such that each distribution has equal area. As shown by Figure 4.17,
the beamline spectrum atop the cavity is similar to the characterized LB-44 spectrum [40] at the
core center and is notably harder than positions away from the CC. Neutron spectra at symmetric
positions (in Fig. 4.16, three meters east, west, or north from the CC) about the CC are very
similar, which is partly a consequence of the simple facility model. The following spectra figures in
this section list the total fluence magnitudes in the legends; these fluences are averaged over a 20
cm diameter tally sphere atop the CC.

 Figure 4.17: HB neutron spectrum atop CC vs. characterized core-level spectrum

Figure 4.18 shows that the neutron spectrum gradually softens with increasing distance from the
beamline. Neutron fluence away from the beamline is downscattered by the hydrogenous ACRRF
materials, including the concrete walls and reactor tank water. Analogous to the dose gradients,
the scattered neutron fluence is three orders of magnitude lower than the beamline fluence.

 Figure 4.18: Gradual softening of HB neutron spectra from beamline

Figure 4.19 compares the spectrum at the CC top with spectra near the roof. Just below the

the dose, fluence magnitudes decrease by an order of magnitude near the roof relative to the CC
top. After attenuation by the steel roof, the spectrum exiting the HB is slightly harder because
lower-energy neutrons are absorbed or scattered away from the tally region.

4.3.3 Beamline Scattering
With a collimated beam of radiation emanating from the CC, it is beneficial to avoid placing
objects in the stream, as doing so can increase HB floor dose by scattering high-energy neutrons
throughout the facility. Hypothetically, plates or other objects might be placed over the cavity
to support experiments and radiation characterization. Scattering effects are analyzed for a 0.39”
aluminum plate, a 1” steel plate, and a 2” lucite block.
The 1” steel plate increases the scattered radiation field around the reactor tank more than the
other objects considered. Figure 4.20 and Figure 4.21 depict mesh TED tallies with the steel plate.
 mrem
Maximum dose on the west side of the reactor tank has increased to ≈100 300 MJ compared to
 mrem
≈30 300 MJ in the standard configuration.

Figure 4.22 shows traces of facility dose for the three objects. In general, beamline scattering by the
objects increases HB dose at the floor level and decreases dose vertically. Even the aluminum plate
noticeably perturbs the lateral radiation field. Vertically, the objects cause a sudden increase in
TED at the cavity exit (4.5 ft over the floor). Above this position, the objects suppress dose. These
results demonstrate that placing certain objects over the CC can make the scattered radiation field
significantly worse.4.7

 Figure 4.22: Scattering impacts on facility dose for different objects on top of the CC

Figure 4.23 and Table 4.5 show how the objects affect the neutron spectrum and fluence magnitude
above the cavity. The magnitudes reflect averaging over a 20 cm diameter sphere that is directly
above the object and the top of the CC tube. The 0.39” aluminum plate has minimal impact
on spectrum but reduces total fluence by ≈20%. The 1” steel plate creates a harder spectrum
above the CC and reduces total fluence by ≈50%. The 2” lucite block thermalizes the spectrum
significantly and reduces total fluence by ≈70%. These effects should be considered when comparing
to measurements.

4.7
 Sufficiently thick shielding material such as polyethylene or lucite can downscatter and absorb fast neutrons locally,
 preventing their scattering throughout the HB.

4.3.4 Facility Scattering: Roof and Walls
The ACRRF model ignores most of the HB internal features such as steel beams for cranes and
piping. More material above the CC can scatter more radiation, increasing HB dose outside the
reactor tank. The ACRRF model also ignores the concrete of surrounding rooms outside the HB.
To approximate the impact of added structures, a modified ACRRF model is used with thicker
concrete walls and a thicker steel roof. An additional meter of concrete is added to the outer
walls, which were originally 20.32 cm (8”) thick. The steel roof is increased in thickness by 5 cm
(originally 2.54 cm thick). Figure 4.24 shows how additional facility materials can increase the
overall HB radiation environment.

                                                  −55−50−45−40−35−30−25−20−15−10 −5 0 5 10 15 20 25                                                                                                                                              −55−50−45−40−35−30−25−20−15−10 −5 0 5 10 15 20 25
 East-west position from cavity [ft] East-west position from cavity [ft]

 (a) Default walls/roof (same as Fig. 4.4) (b) Thick walls and roof

 Figure 4.24: Elevation TED mesh tallies showing impact of additional facility scattering

 Figure 4.25 shows facility scattering effects on east-west and south-north TED profiles. Included is
 a case with no facility walls and no roof, which benefits personnel dose in the HB via reduced back-
 scattering. Naturally, the lack of facility walls increases dose beyond the ACRRF domain. The
 beamline dose is not substantially altered by the different facility models. Beyond 10 ft from the
 CC, the thicker walls and roof increase dose by ≈1.5–2X over the default facility model. Scattering
 impacts the dose around local or ad-hoc “shielding” like the tissue phantom. With the facility
 walls, scattering limits the efficacy of local shielding (i.e., radiation just scatters around it). With
 reduced scattering (i.e., no walls), dose behind the phantom is significantly lower than at the face
 of the phantom towards the CC.

 68

 longer being attenuated. In the base MCNP model, the roof is approximated as a 1” steel plate,

 4.3.5 Effective Dose Coefficients
 Different fluence-to-dose conversion tables were analyzed in the beamline in Section 4.3.1. Facility-
 level results using the default ROT ICRP-74 (with ANS–6.1.1–1991 for gamma dose) tables are
 compared to AP and isotropic tables from ICRP-116. Rotational tables from ICRP-116 produce
 results very similar to the default tables. Figure 4.26 shows that AP dose is ≈2× greater than
 isotropic dose at positions throughout the HB, including the beamline. Combined with the effects
 of additional scattering (Section 4.3.3 and Section 4.3.4), different fluence-to-dose tables can change
 HB dose by a factor of ≈4×. The use of older dose tables, such as those from ANS–6.1.1–1977,
 produces higher dose similar to AP dose.

5 SUPPLEMENTARY SCENARIOS
Additional scenarios are analyzed, including partially shielded HB dose with concurrent NRS
leakage, aircraft dose, and dose using manually prescribed source information.

5.1 Partially Shielded HB Dose with NRS
Auxiliary analyses of partially shielded CC and NRS radiation outputs are presented. For normal,
fully shielded operations, the NRS is a significant source of HB radiation. The NRS, although
not ringed with fuel like FREC-II, provides a large streaming path to the HB (3× the CC cross-
sectional area), and the NRS SP may not be as effective as the CC and FREC-II SPs. Therefore,
an integral analysis of CC and NRS radiation leakage is warranted. Although results with the NRS
show notable increases in dose to the north of the CC (i.e., near the NRS tube), the dominant HB
dose feature is still CC radiation beaming through the 4” insert hole.
Relative to the scenarios in Section 3, analyses in this section include the partial CC SP, the
LB-44 bucket, and the NRS collimator. An alternative SSW-SSR approach is also explored as a
supplementary calculation.
5.1.1 ACRRF MCNP Model with NRS
A high-fidelity MCNP representation of the NRS model is integrated into the ACRRF model.
Figure 5.1 shows the MCNP model of the partially shielded ACRR—with the LB-44 bucket resting
on the 32” pedestal—and the high-fidelity NRS. For these analyses, the collimator is present in the
NRS. Further description of the NRS model and collimator materials may be found in Section 3
and previous work [31, 32, 33]. A facility-level diagram of the model is depicted in Figure 5.2. The
shielded cells are still present but are not visible in the 30-degree NRS viewing plane shown in
Fig. 5.2.

 (a) Cross-section view

Figure 5.6. The TED figures compare partially shielded dose including the NRS (with collimator) to
original results (from Section 4) that only consider the CC. Tissue phantoms are included in these
cases. Even when partially shielded by the collimator, the NRS causes noticeable dose increases
 mrem mrem
near the northwest region of the reactor tank, doubling the local dose from 30 300 MJ to 60 300 MJ .
However, the overall HB dose field remains dominated by CC output. With the collimator present,
the partially shielded CC dose is approximately 100 times greater than the NRS dose.

The DXTRAN-WW method is not used for the partially shielded NRS scenario. Thus, the usual
beamline statistical anomalies are present in the NRS radiation figures above. These anomalies
also increase the errors on cell track-length tallies in Table 5.1. Due to the nuances of the PSC
parameter (see Appendix A.3), a surface source cannot be used reliably with DXTRAN when multiple
radiation outlets are present. The angular distributions of neutron output from the CC and NRS
are distinct, and the code accepts only a single PSC input value. The photon outputs for each cavity
likewise have distinct angular distributions.

 75

To improve the partially shielded CC-NRS beamline solution and address the PSC issue, a cell-
based SSW-SSR approach is explored. Using a cell source (i.e., SSW CEL), fission neutron and photon
births are written to the SSW file. In the SSR calculation, DXTRAN or point detectors can be used by
simply specifying PSC=0.5 because fission is isotropic [4, 10]. A major challenge remains, however:
particles follow a much more tortuous path to escape through the vertical cavities. VR techniques
must therefore be refined to avoid wasting effort tracking particles deep in the core and pool that
never contribute meaningfully to HB dose.
Figure 5.7 compares the HB dose profiles from the surface- and cell-based SSW calculations. With
the added use of DXTRAN-WWs, the cell-based approach improves beamline resolution at the expense
of resolution in the remainder of the problem domain. Although refinements to the VR inputs might
yield improvements, VR techniques often improve some portions of phase space at the expense of
others.

5.2 Low-Flying Aircraft Dose Estimates
Long-range and statistically resolved MCNP dose calculations are feasible using VR techniques.
In conjunction with SSW-SSR, DXTRAN and WWs enable dose calculations at distances thousands
of feet from the facility. In this section, dose from the partially shielded CC is evaluated for the
airspace surrounding the ACRRF, with radiation from the FREC-II and NRS neglected.
To facilitate dose calculations at aircraft altitudes (1000–3000 ft above ACRRF for a flight to/from

 76

the Sunport), neutron energy splitting5.1 and a higher photon energy cutoff5.2 are implemented.
An overhead aircraft would have a speed over 100 knots, minimizing exposure time directly over
the facility. Hence, the potential for prompt, reactor-driven dose is only a concern for large pulses.
Because the extent of the model can be important for radiation scattering in the air (sky-shine),
8 km of airspace above the ACRRF is modeled. Dose tallies are specified that extend to 3000
ft (≈0.9 km) above the facility. Table 5.2 provides the assumed density distribution of air as a
function of height over the ACRRF. 8 km of surrounding earth outside the facility is also modeled
to approximate ground-shine effects.5.3

 Table 5.2: Air density for aircraft dose calculations

 Altitude Density g/cm−3
                                            0–250 m               1.0245·10−3
                                            250–500 m              1.015·10−3
                                            500–750 m              9.96·10−4
                                            750–1000 m             9.68·10−4
                                            1–1.25 km              9.41·10−4
                                            1.25–1.5 km            9.15·10−4
                                            1.5–1.75 km            8.90·10−4
                                            1.75–2 km              8.65·10−4
                                            2–3 km                 8.41·10−4
                                            3–4 km                 7.70·10−4
                                            4–5 km                 6.90·10−4
                                            5–8 km                 6.00·10−4

Figure 5.8 shows TED above the facility; this calculation uses the small DXTRAN sphere shown in
Fig. 4.3. With the 4” insert absent from the SP, an anisotropic radiation beam extends hundreds
to thousands of feet above the ACRRF roof, a trend corroborated by alternative source modeling
in Section 5.3. Naturally, dose magnitudes are much lower than those in the HB. The dashed white
lines signify a cone with a half angle of 1 degree. These results are from a cylindrical mesh tally
averaged over 3 ft axial and radial zones. Due to azimuthal tally integration, perfect symmetry
exists on either side (east-west) of the facility.5.4 The associated relative error over the mesh is
provided by Figure 5.9. Through the use of VR techniques and judicious sizing of the tally mesh,
most of the airspace exhibits less than 1% stochastic error. Error increases over 1% in regions
⪆2000 ft over the facility.

Figure 5.10 shows TED as a function of altitude with the base-case roof (1” steel) and with no

roof.5.5 Passengers aboard an aircraft 1000 ft directly above the ACRRF during a maximum pulse
 mrem

 mrem
2000 ft, TED is less than 0.1 300 MJ . Stochastic error in the beamline becomes apparent above
≈2400 ft, which can be further improved by running more histories or additional VR techniques.
This study demonstrates that direct MCNP estimates of prompt, long-distance ACRR dose are
feasible. Estimates are statistically reliable and technically defensible with direct consideration of
a reactor source consistent with characterized institutional models.

5.3 Manual Source Approach
Manual user-defined source calculations are performed to explore the HB and aircraft dose magnitudes
and gradients. The manual source calculations are limited to the partially shielded configuration,
which includes the standalone ACRR, the shield plug, and no 4” insert. These analyses demonstrate
how the complexity of the collimated reactor source is best handled automatically by MCNP’s SSW-
SSR capability.
Given the statistical challenges of KCODE calculations (and even of SSW-SSR calculations), it is
tempting to avoid direct consideration of the source distribution and uncertainty. Ideally, educated
guessing and a manually constructed source could reproduce the SSW-SSR results without the
statistical anomalies. Two such potential approaches are:
 • Considering the shielding and collimation provided by ≈7 m of water, assume that radiation
 escaping the cavity creates a unidirectional source with all particles traveling upward.
 • Because fission is isotropic, assume the source is an isotropic point or volume source. The
 cavity fluence is known to be rotationally symmetric [7], lending credence to an isotropic
 source approximation.

5.5
 Due to averaging over the mesh zones, dose magnitudes in Fig. 5.10 are slightly lower than “point” estimates over
 a 30-cm diameter tally sphere located 1,000 feet over ACRRF.

 79

While both of these approaches seem rational, they clearly entail different treatments of the source
angular distribution. Without the SSW-SSR results as a baseline, it is difficult, if not impossible, to
judge which approximation is better. A third treatment could assume that the angular distribution
is neither isotropic nor unidirectional, but somewhere in between. For example, a surface source
could be defined to emit particles upward with a cosine distribution, or a manual histogram could
be specified based on a KCODE tally.
These manual approaches were tested in the ACRRF model and compared to previous results using
SSW-SSR. Manual source definitions were created of a unidirectional disc source, an isotropic source
over a thin disc volume, and an upward surface source with a cosine distribution. In each case, the
source magnitude was informed by an F1:N tally, which records the number of neutrons crossing
the same surface used in the DXTRAN-WW calculations described in Section 4 (see Fig. 4.3). The
energy spectrum is also recorded over an 89-group structure.
As shown by Figure 5.11, the unidirectional source overpredicts the SSW-SSR results by orders of
magnitude, and the isotropic distribution underpredicts the SSW-SSR results by factors of 20–50×.
The cosine distribution is closest to the SSW-SSR results. The true angular distribution is more
collimated, however, since the cosine HB doses are still 5–15× lower than the SSW-SSR results.
Figure 5.12 compares SSW-SSR and cosine results.

directly treat all characteristics of the source. This table and the mesh tallies above demonstrate
the importance of modeling the source correctly. Without the SSW-SSR results, wildly different
dose estimates can be made that each appear reasonable and have low stochastic error. As always,
model error and uncertainty dwarf the code-estimated Monte Carlo uncertainty.

The angular distributions of cavity neutron current are tallied just above the core and farther
upward above the shield plug (with the insert removed). Figure 5.15 shows normalized neutron
current, where 0 degrees corresponds to the upward cavity direction. Even just above the core,
≈50% of neutrons travel within 20 degrees of the surface normal, and most of the remainder are
within 40 degrees. Above the SP, ≈98% of neutrons travel within 5 degrees of the surface normal.
Although a poor approximation for the source below the SP, a unidirectional manual source above

 82

the SP, with appropriately reduced magnitude, might provide a reasonable approximation to the
true source.

 Figure 5.16: Angular distributions: focus on 0 to 20 degrees (0=straight up the CC)

These angular distributions could be implemented in MCNP SDEF records to improve upon the
manual source distribution estimates. Finer F1 tallies with additional bins could, in principle,
provide better information for a fixed-source calculation. As spectral, spatial, and angular bins
are added, however, minimizing statistical error across the tally bins becomes arduous in a KCODE
calculation. Generating the corresponding SDEF input record also becomes more complex. Taken to
the extreme, this approach may effectively recreate MCNP’s SSW-SSR capability, with the exception
of automatic error propagation. External workarounds to the error-propagation issue are still
conceivable, such as multiple SDEF definitions that encapsulate uncertainty. Avoiding such complicated
workarounds is a primary advantage of the SSW-SSR approach.

 83

 84

6 CONCLUSION
Parts A and B of this work have documented the use of MCNP in generating statistically reliable
evaluations of radiation exposure in the ACRRF HB due to prompt reactor outputs. Part A
provided base SSW-SSR results for several configurations—these calculations (rightfully) neglected
the NRS and FREC-II to prioritize validation of CC radiation outputs. Validation of beamline
dose in Part A revealed good agreement between calculated and measured values. Part B provided
complementary analyses including a verification study in Section 2. V&V of the ACRR radiation
output study is thereby complete but remains to be improved. Additional V&V studies would
benefit model applicability on the roof and outside the beamline.
To inform the ACRRF safety basis, Section 3 of Part B provided a calculation of maximum HB dose
due to simultaneous output from the CC, FREC-II, and NRS. Each vertical cavity was unshielded,
and AP fluence-to-dose coefficients were used—dose from AP coefficients generally exceed ROT-
 rem rem
derived values by a factor of 2×. Maximum dose was ≈1430 300 MJ or ≈40,930 hr for prolonged
exposure to full-power, steady-state operation. Given the size of FREC-II, it produced the greatest
HB dose when coupled to the ACRR. Unshielded dose atop the CC for this configuration was
 rem rem
≈620 300  MJ (≈17,840 hr ). For an unshielded NRS tube with the collimator absent, NRS dose
 rem rem
was relatively minimal at ≈80 300 MJ (≈2,290 hr ). However, the proximity of the NRS to the
reactor tank wall is an important consideration. For potential personnel exposure at practical
positions on the HB floor, the NRS can be a significant source of local radiation.
The novel application of SSW-SSR methodology in both parts of this work enabled statistical
sampling of the HB that would be impractical with KCODE calculations. As applied here, the
SSW-SSR approach focuses on the particles most important to prompt, reactor-driven HB dose;
these particle tracks are replicated many times (typically ≳ 105 times each) using unique random
number seeds. In this manner, much of the facility and surrounding airspace is well resolved on
a spatial grid comprising 2.5–5 cm zones, and 89-group neutron spectral tallies are feasible when
sampled over a generously sized tally region (e.g., a 20-cm-diameter sphere).
Despite the overall effectiveness of the SSW-SSR approach, shielded and partially shielded configurat-
ions entail statistical anomalies (i.e., beamline weight fluctuations) that cannot be alleviated through
SSR track replication. In Section 4 of Part B, the additional use of DXTRAN and WWs in the SSR
step was very effective in suppressing these anomalies. With these additional VR techniques, highly
resolved spatial (≈1 cm grid) and spectral details were calculated in Section 4.3. Statistically
resolved reaction tallies over 640 energy groups will be useful for further characterization efforts.
Caveats associated with the DXTRAN-WW results were also noted.
Section 4.3 presented select model sensitivities. Different fluence-to-dose coefficients were assessed.
Variations in scattering of the CC beamline were explored for hypothetical objects placed over the
cavity and for different facility modeling assumptions (e.g., roof and walls). Several other modeling
features and options, such as internal ACRRF structural details, are capable of influencing predicted
HB doses, and rigorous uncertainty studies are reserved for future work. A conclusion was reached—
consistent with the safety basis analysis in Section 3—that predicted TED can readily vary by a
factor of two depending on model assumptions.
Supplementary calculations using DXTRAN-WWs in Section 5 were provided for aircraft dose above
ACRRF. With the added VR techniques, tally statistics remain reliable thousands of feet from the
 mrem
facility, and the potential aircraft dose was determined to be 1–2 300 MJ . Additional DXTRAN-WW
results were presented for partially shielded CC and NRS radiation outputs, in which the CC SP
was in place without the 4” insert, and the collimator was used as a surrogate for the NRS SP.

 85

Consistent with the analyses in Section 3, dose atop the NRS is much lower than the peak CC dose;
however, the position of the NRS, being near the tank wall, is still of practical significance for HB
personnel exposure.
Materials in the Part B appendices include confirmatory analyses of the modeled reactor source and
the SSW-SSR approach, tables of fluence-to-dose coefficients, and a well-resolved 640-group neutron
spectrum for the partially shielded CC.
To recap the main deductions from Parts A and B, Table 6.1 provides calculated ACRRF TED
values for partially shielded and unshielded configurations. Dose values exceeding 100 rem are
rounded to the nearest 5 rem. The results in Table 6.1 span the key configurations and scenarios
considered in both Parts A and B of this research.
