 tube.

Gross modeling sensitivities and potential sources of unknown error generally dwarf the low statistical
uncertainties in both parts of this work. System configuration, such as the presence of shielding and
coupling with external cavities, and the exposure basis (i.e., dose per pulse or per MW-hr) have large
impacts on HB dose. Mitigative actions, including the LB-44 bucket and administrative restrictions
on pulse size, steady-state power level, and exposure time, are also important considerations. Thus,
even with good agreement between calculation and measurement (≲ 30%, based on Table 2.5 from
Part A), the selection of a single value for potential HB personnel exposure is partly arbitrary.
Ultimately, expert judgment may be most useful when considered alongside the results of these
reports.

APPENDIX A REACTOR SOURCE MODELING
The reactor sources used in this work are briefly analyzed to demonstrate the following:
  • The reactor models are reasonable and consistent with established Sandia understanding and
 characterization.
  • The SSW-SSR method correctly treats reactor radiation traveling through vertical ACRRF
 cavities, enabling reliable estimates of HB dose.
  • The nuances of the SSR PSC input parameter when using partially deterministic methods like
 DXTRAN.

A.1 Neutronics and Core-level Cavity Radiation
Figures of the MCNP reactor model geometries and materials were provided in the main sections
of the report. Fig. 3.1 showed the ACRR–FREC-II–NRS system with free-field cavities. Fig. 5.1
showed the ACRR–NRS system with LB-44 in the CC. These are the primary reactor configurations
examined in this work.
Part A focused on the standalone ACRR and neglected the FREC-II and NRS. Part A included a
model pedigree study demonstrating the standalone ACRR model reproduces characterized reactor
cavity radiation metrics [6, 7]. Therefore, the reactor analyses here establish the pedigree of the
coupled ACRR–FREC-II system. While the NRS is included in these analyses, it has minimal
neutronic worth and very minor impacts on power peaking [33].
Figure A.1 shows peaking-factor distributions for the main reactor configurations. Fig. A.1a,
Fig. A.1b, and Fig. A.1c show distributions for the FF reactor system described in Section 3.
In Fig. A.1a, the FREC-II is coupled, the FRs are raised, and the CRs are near delayed critical,
resulting in 63% of the coupled energy being generated by the ACRR and 37% by the FREC-II.
As the CRs are positioned higher for large pulses, the coupled peaking distribution shifts toward
the ACRR. With the CRs positioned for a $3.5 pulse, a 75% ACRR to 25% FREC-II energy split
is typical [13, 41]; as Figure A.2 shows, this work’s reactor model replicates this trend. Shielded
buckets in the FREC-II, or lowering of the FRs, can shift the coupled system even more toward the
ACRR (i.e., the ACRR generates ≳ 90% of the energy). The safety basis calculations in Section 3
deliberately analyzed a coupled system with a higher, but still realistic, FREC-II energy fraction
to drive more radiation out through the larger FREC-II tube.
Fig. A.1b shows peaking factors for the ACRR and FREC-II when the FREC-II is decoupled,
tilted away, the FRs are lowered, and the eastern nickel plate is inserted. In this configuration, the
neutronic impact of the FREC-II is relatively small, and essentially all nuclear energy is generated
by the ACRR. The peaking factors shown in this figure are therefore somewhat misleading.
Accordingly, Fig. A.1c shows peaking for the same configuration but focuses on the ACRR. ACRR
peaking factors in Fig. A.1c are consistent with characterized reactor models [42, 7, 43, 15].
Maximum peaking in the FF ACRR is typically ≈1.5 and occurs in the second hexagonal fuel
row, adjacent to a raised TR.
Fig. A.1d shows power peaking for the standalone ACRR model with the LB-44 bucket. The
general peaking trends are again consistent with previous Sandia reactor modeling [6, 15]. This
model was used for the partially shielded dose calculations in Section 4 and Section 5. The LB-44

 91

suppresses power peaking in the innermost row of fuel, and its negative reactivity worth (around –
$6) necessitates higher CR positions for reactor operation; both effects flatten the power distribution
and reduce the maximum peaking factor to ≈1.43.

The following figures illustrate the prompt-radiation source of the ACRR–FREC-II system, in which
the FREC-II is analyzed in coupled and decoupled states. When FREC-II is decoupled, it remains

 93

present in the model but is tilted away from the ACRR, its FRs are lowered, and the eastern nickel
plate is in place. When FREC-II is coupled, the FRs are raised. The NRS is also present in each
case with its collimator.
  • Figure A.3 shows radial views of thermal and total neutron fluence on a linear scale.
  • Figure A.4 shows side views—through the FREC-II plane—of thermal, fast, and total neutron
 fluence on a logarithmic scale.
  • Figure A.5 shows side views—through the NRS plane—of neutron fluence.
  • Figure A.6 shows radial views of effective neutron and gamma dose.
  • Figure A.7 shows side views of TED.
  • Figure A.8 shows different views of absorbed dose in tissue where the FREC-II is coupled.
Typical and reasonable ACRR and FREC-II neutronic characteristics are exhibited by the reactor
model used in this work. Thermal fluence is lower on the ACRR side of the system. Thermal
neutron absorption in the nickel structures surrounding the ACRR is evident. Thermal fluence
is greatest around the FREC-II, which lacks nickel (or other mildly thermal-neutron-absorbing)
reflector elements, and whose hydrided UZrH fuel produces a more thermalized spectrum than
the ACRR. Fast fluence, total fluence, and dose metrics are 5–10× greater in the ACRR. When
FREC-II is coupled, especially with its FRs raised and the ACRR CRs positioned near critical,
the FREC-II perturbs the coupled power distribution, decreasing radiation magnitudes in the
ACRR. When FREC-II is decoupled, radiation magnitudes in the FREC-II decrease by an order
of magnitude and remain slightly greater than those in the NRS. Core-level neutron dose, both
effective and absorbed, exceeds gamma dose by ∼ 40×. Given the strategic levels of neutron fluence
generated by the ACRR, core-level neutron dose is on the order of 107 rad[tissue] 8  rem
                                                                       300 MJ and 10 300 MJ .

A.2 Source Representation by SSW-SSR
Different SSW-SSR treatments were investigated, including the location and extent of the surface-
source definition. For partially shielded CC radiation outputs, the SSW-SSR results documented in
Parts A and B used a surface below the SP that restricted recording particles to those traveling
upward through the cavity—particles in the pool were neglected. Thus, it is prudent to examine
how particles traveling upward in the pool may contribute to HB dose.
TED mesh tallies derived from a surface-source that includes all upward traveling particles (including
those in the pool) are shown in Figure A.9 and Figure A.10. These results are compared to the
default approach in this work, which neglects the pool tracks. The beamline and overall HB dose
are unaffected by the extent of the surface-source definition. Note that the pool tracks considered
here still neglect radiation traveling upward in the NRS tube or FREC-II.

of neutron fluence and geometry surfaces. Figure A.12 shows processed TED tallies. Each figure
has three subfigures: the left subfigure is from the SSW calculation, the middle subfigure is a SSR
calculation that includes pool and CC tracks, and the right subfigure is a SSR calculation that
only considers CC tracks. (In the SSR calculations, the core region is removed and specified to be

Radiation escaping through the dry cavity is very consistent across the SSW and SSR calculations.
Naturally, the case that ignores pool tracks underestimates pool radiation levels just above the core.
However, pool radiation ≈11 ft above the bottom grid plate is very similar between the middle (all
tracks) and right subfigures (no pool tracks). Considering the much lower radiation magnitude in
the pool water (≈1 rem vs. ⪆10,000 rem in the CC), the pool tracks can be reasonably neglected.
The reactor pool is an effective radiation shield. Given the depth of the reactor pool (≈7 m)

compared to the CC diameter (a ratio of ≈30), HB radiation is dominated by particles traversing
uncollided through the CC and the SP insert hole. Venerable reactor shielding references support
this deduction [2, 27]. For neutrons entering the pool and not the CC, fluence plots like Fig. A.11a
show that most neutrons are absorbed within 30 cm of the core. Statistically, few neutrons reach
out 1 m into the pool from the core region. As these neutrons are downscattered and absorbed in
the water, gamma rays are generated 1–2 m away from the core. Gamma rays generated in the
pool would still have to survive many collisions through the remaining ≈4 m of the pool before
reaching the HB. A 1 MeV gamma ray has a mean free path of ≈10 cm in water [44].

A.3 SSR PSC Parameter and Gamma Dose
For MCNP SSR calculations utilizing partially deterministic methods, the user must specify an
angular source parameter (PSC) on the SSR card. With sufficient particle-dependent information on
phase space distributions over the SSW surface, the PSC parameter could be directly calculated or
handled by a user-supplied source subroutine. SSW-SSR calculations typically avoid such complicated
tasks by design.
A constant PSC value of 15.9 is specified in calculations using DXTRAN. Because the neutron
component dominates TED in unshielded or partially shielded configurations, this PSC value was
chosen to obtain neutron effective dose at the top of the CC that most closely matches the KCODE
results, which are statistically equivalent to the base SSW-SSR results without DXTRAN. Due to
different angular distributions of neutrons and gamma rays crossing the SSW surface, neutron and
gamma dose in the HB exhibit unique “scaling” behavior as PSC is varied, and MCNP only accepts
one PSC value for all particles read from the SSW file. Using PSC=15.9, gamma dose in the beamline is
overestimated relative to KCODE and base case results that do not utilize DXTRAN. For conservatism,
this potential overestimation of gamma dose is included in the DXTRAN-WW results including the
aircraft dose estimates.
Figure 4.12 shows how the PSC parameter acts as a linear multiplier on dose above the cavity
and near the roof. Here, DXTRAN-WW dose results are relative to KCODE results. Using a value of
PSC=15.9, neutron dose at both the cavity and roof agree well with the KCODE results. However,
DXTRAN-WW gamma dose at the cavity is ≈4× greater than the KCODE gamma dose. Near the
roof, the gamma dose overestimation could be a factor of ≈4–5×—greater statistical error in the
KCODE-derived roof gamma dose makes this determination more difficult.

 (a) Atop cavity (b) Just below HB roof

 Figure A.13: Neutron and gamma ratios relative to KCODE results as a function of PSC

 The PSC issue is an important caveat when using partially deterministic methods in SSR calculations.
 For this reason, DXTRAN should be used alongside the conventional SSW-SSR workflow (i.e., omit
 DXTRAN and point detectors). Nonetheless, TED for the partially shielded configuration is dominated
 by neutron dose, and overestimation of gamma dose can be regarded as an added conservatism.
 Four ways to address the gamma PSC issue are:
 1. Define a cell “surface source” (SSW CEL), which records fission sites in the fuel cells. Set
 PSC=0.5 because fission is isotropic.
 2. Turn off DXTRAN for photons.
 3. Keep DXTRAN on for photons, but only read neutrons from the SSW (PTY=N on the SSR card).
 As usual, use coupled neutron and gamma (MODE N P) transport.
 4. Perform separate gamma calculations for the “direct” component of gamma dose (PTY=P and
 MODE P) using a PSC value tailored for photon transport. To obtain total dose, sum the direct
 gamma dose with the neutron and indirect gamma doses from the third option above. Both
 direct and indirect gamma calculations use DXTRAN for photons.
 A cell-based surface source was successfully generated, but it resulted in relatively slow and
 ineffective SSR calculations and was therefore not pursued further for standalone CC calculations.
 However, the cell-based surface source was explored further for the partially shielded NRS outputs
 in Section 5.1, where it showed some promising initial results.
 Turning off DXTRAN for photons addressed the PSC issue but increased the relative error in gamma
 dose considerably, which is undesirable. Results for this option are not provided here.
 The third option (PTY=N) also helps with the PSC issue but neglects gamma rays streaming directly
 from the surface source. Gamma dose directly in the beam was consequently underestimated,
 while dose away from the cavity (due to neutron-collision-spawned photons) was largely similar
 to results that read neutrons and photons from the SSW (PTY=N P), as shown in Figure A.14.
 Neutron-dominated TED in the beamline and around the facility are also very similar, as shown in
 Figure A.15. The specification of photon DXTRAN and the scope of particles read from the surface
 source have no substantive impact on TED when the 4” insert is removed. For expediency, the
 fourth option in the numbered list above was not attempted for this work. If the HB dose metrics of

 102

concern were more sensitive to gamma radiation, the fourth option would probably be the necessary
and best approach.

APPENDIX B FLUENCE-TO-DOSE CONVERSION COEFFICIENTS

dose, both with ROT irradiation geometry. Table B.1 and Table B.2 list these dose functions [16, 4].

ICRP-116 AP tables for neutron and gamma dose [17]. ICRP-116 AP coefficients are listed in
Table B.3 and Table B.4.

APPENDIX C 640 GROUP NEUTRON OUTPUT SPECTRUM ATOP CC
A 640-group spectrum of neutron fluence atop the CC is calculated using the SSW-SSR-DXTRAN-WW
method from Section 4. The spectrum is well-resolved with bin errors <10% for neutron energies
between 9x10−9 MeV (≈0.01 eV) and 7.6 MeV. Bins with errors exceeding 10% are highlighted in
Table C.1—these bins have trivial fluence compared to the total fluence over the spectrum. This
table lists neutron fluence, the bin fraction of total fluence (summing to 1.0), fluence per lethargy,
and relative error for each bin. Total fluence atop the CC is ≈5.1x107 when calculated using
DXTRAN-WW and a 20-cm-diameter tally sphere.
